## Chapter 1: The Pembroke NST Interview System

[[#Contents]]

Pembroke College occupies a distinctive position in Cambridge undergraduate admissions: it is one of the larger and more heavily subscribed of the standard undergraduate colleges, and its Natural Sciences interviews are correspondingly competitive.

**No college-level offer rate is quoted in this report.** Figures such as a "13.2% offer rate" circulate widely in applicant forums and secondary summaries, but they are not published by the college, they move substantially year to year and across subjects, and they are not comparable between colleges — an applicant interviewed at one college may receive an offer from another via the Winter Pool, so a low college offer rate can reflect pooling rather than selectivity. Check the current cycle against the University's published admissions statistics and Pembroke's own admissions pages before relying on any ranking.

What is robust — and what this report is built on — is the *structure* of the interview, not a percentage. Understanding that structure, its logic, and its preferences is the essential first step towards effective preparation.

### 1.1 Interview Structure: The Distinctive 'Preparation Time' Mechanism

Pembroke's Natural Sciences interviews follow the unified Cambridge framework whilst possessing one reportedly distinctive college characteristic — **interview preparation time** (interview prep). Approximately 30 to 40 minutes before the formal interview, the applicant is taken to a private space and given a set of problems based on A-Level Mathematics and Further Mathematics, some of which may contain material beyond the syllabus that the applicant has never encountered. During this period, you are expected to think independently, work through calculations, and leave behind written workings. **Evidence status: applicant-reported, not college-published.** Accounts of this mechanism can be traced back to at least 2019 and were still being reported by applicants in the 2023 cycle. Neither Pembroke College nor the University's admissions pages describe such a procedure, and interview formats are reviewed annually, so it is not safe to generalise from individual reports to a college-wide rule. Treat it as a well-attested *possibility* to prepare for — practising unstructured problems under time pressure is valuable either way — rather than as a guaranteed feature of your own interview.

The formal interview typically consists of two sessions, each lasting roughly 20 to 30 minutes, conducted by two interviewers in a panel format. The panel normally includes the college's Admissions Tutor, the relevant Director of Studies (DoS), other Fellows in the discipline, and occasionally doctoral students. For the physical sciences, the Part IA Physical NST DoS is Professor Mark Wyatt of the Institute of Astronomy, with Dr F. Lee serving as DoS for Physics; for chemistry, Dr Stephanie Smith holds the DoS role. These supervisors are, in most cases, the very people who will conduct your supervisions — which is why the interview is, at its core, a two-way 'teaching match'.

### 1.2 What They Look For: The Assessment Framework

Pembroke interviews are intensely academic. The core assessment dimensions fall into five categories:

**Academic potential** — can you thrive under Cambridge's supervision system? This goes well beyond 'can you solve the problem'. It is about whether, when faced with an unfamiliar question, you can rapidly construct an analytical framework.

**Thinking process** — particularly the ability to think aloud. Interviewers do not expect you to produce the correct answer immediately; they expect to hear your reasoning, to see how you dismantle a problem, test assumptions, and correct errors as you go.

**Teachability** — when the interviewer offers a hint, can you take it and move forward? This is the essence of the supervision model: the supervisor points in a direction, and the student walks the rest of the way.

**Intellectual curiosity and passion** — have you read beyond the syllabus? Can you discuss the frontiers of the discipline? Is there genuine interest in the world beyond the textbook?

**Interdisciplinary integration** — this is the assessment dimension unique to NST. The first year of the Natural Sciences Tripos requires every student to study three sciences plus one mathematics course. Cross-disciplinary thinking is not an elective; it is compulsory. Interviewers will deliberately test your reasoning ability in subjects you do not intend to specialise in.

For the physical sciences, interviews centre on conceptual mechanics, Fermi estimation, derivation from first principles, graph sketching, and dimensional analysis — with mathematical confidence serving as the core threshold. Chemistry interviews lean towards reaction mechanisms (explaining electron flow), a deep — rather than rote-memorised — understanding of periodic trends, multi-route thinking in organic synthesis, and the flexible application of fundamental physical chemistry concepts.

### 1.3 Admissions Data and Thresholds

Pembroke imposes subject-combination and A\* distribution requirements that are stricter than those of most colleges: the typical offer is A\*A\*A, with the explicit stipulation that the A\* grades must fall in Mathematics (or Further Mathematics) and either Chemistry or Physics. The published Cambridge profile for **Natural Sciences (Physical)** entrants in recent cycles shows that 97% achieved A\*A\*A\* or above, 100% take Mathematics, 97% take Physics, 92% take Further Mathematics, and 89% take Chemistry — of those taking Chemistry, 81% also take Physics.

**Note the scope.** That 97% is the **Physical** stream, not Natural Sciences as a whole: the corresponding figure for Natural Sciences (Biological) entrants is 76%, while the published *minimum* offer for both streams is A\*A\*A. (Source: University of Cambridge undergraduate course pages for Natural Sciences, 2025 entry cycle, entrant-qualifications data. Treat the subject percentages as indicative and re-check the current cycle before quoting them.)

### 1.4 Written Assessment and Interview Format

The written assessment is the gateway to interview. For **2025 entry** (first sittings in October 2024), the NSAA (Natural Sciences Admissions Assessment) was replaced by the ESAT (Engineering and Science Admissions Test). The ESAT is not four interchangeable modules: it comprises Mathematics 1 (compulsory for every candidate), Mathematics 2, Biology, Chemistry and Physics, and Natural Sciences applicants sit Mathematics 1 plus two further modules as specified for their course. The ESAT is used to shortlist applicants for interview, and interviewers may draw on ESAT performance for targeted follow-up questioning. Cambridge publishes no weighting formula, so resist the comforting assumption that a strong interview 'cancels out' a weak test score: the defensible reading is that interview performance forms an independent part of the assessment alongside the test, the school reference, predicted grades and contextual data.

On interview format, Pembroke has in recent cycles appeared in Cambridge's 'differentiated by applicant location' category. **Check the current year rather than relying on this paragraph:** the University's published interview-format table for the most recent verifiable cycle lists Pembroke as UK applicants in person and overseas applicants online, and does *not* describe an overseas choice between the two. The format is set college by college and reviewed annually, so quote the current table (and your own interview invitation, once it arrives) as the authority. Interviews are concentrated in the first three weeks of December each year, with invitations typically issued in November.

### 1.5 Recent Developments and Trends

Several changes over the past decade merit attention. Beyond the written-assessment reform and the hybridisation of interview formats, the 2020 pandemic led Pembroke, in rare instances, to issue offers without interview — a historically exceptional departure for the college. The number of mainland Chinese applicants **university-wide** has grown steadily — widely reported as about 2,900 in the 2025 cycle, roughly a third of international applications — while the reported share eventually taking up places has drifted downwards (a commonly quoted figure of 9.8% is an *acceptance* rate, not an offer rate). **Read these as University-level, not Pembroke-level:** no college publishes a Chinese-applicant series, and offer rate and acceptance rate are different quantities that secondary reporting routinely conflates. The direction of travel is what matters for your preparation: competition continues to intensify.

### 1.6 A Panoramic View of Cambridge College Interview Characteristics

Beyond Pembroke, each Cambridge college brings its own flavour to NST interviews. Understanding these differences helps situate Pembroke within the wider Cambridge landscape.

**Evidence status:** the descriptions below are compiled from applicant reports and admissions-guidance summaries for recent cycles. They are not college-published rules, several have changed from year to year, and none should be treated as a guarantee for your own cycle. Verify against each college's current admissions page and the University's published interview-format table.

- **Trinity College**: Also employs a pre-interview preparation paper; applicants work through problems beforehand and discuss their answers during the interview.
- **St John's College**: Uses an at-interview test, typically four long questions completed in one hour, with the mathematics section often structured as a series of sub-questions.
- **King's College**: Known for cross-disciplinary reasoning; interviewers switch between subjects during questioning.
- **Corpus Christi College**: Applicants may choose two chemistry interviews or two physics interviews, rather than one of each — an advantage for those with a pronounced subject preference.
- **Emmanuel College**: Sends preparation materials in advance and requires physics applicants to declare a preferred area (Physics or Chemistry).
- **Gonville & Caius College**: Allows applicants to select an interview focus between Chemistry and Physics.
- **Churchill College**: its Physics DoS, Lisa Jardine-Wright, is co-director of the Isaac Physics platform. **Caution:** claims that Churchill requires in-person interviews for *all* applicants circulate widely, but Cambridge's published interview-format table for the most recent verifiable cycle did not list Churchill among the in-person exceptions. Interview format is set college by college and reviewed annually — check the current published table rather than any secondary summary.
- **Sidney Sussex College**: Has published two NST mock interview videos on YouTube — officially recommended preparation resources.

## Chapter 2: Comprehensive Question Analysis and Preparation Strategy

[[#Contents]]

This chapter is built on a systematic classification of 207 Cambridge NST interview questions — 84 Physics, 60 Chemistry, 63 NST Mathematics — to distil the underlying logic of question-setting, the distribution of high-frequency topics, and an effective preparation strategy. **A note on the counts:** the three subject totals (84 + 60 + 63) sum exactly to 207, but the topic tallies in §2.2 are *not* mutually exclusive — a Fermi-estimation question, for instance, is tagged under both physics and mathematics — and they do not exhaust the corpus. Read them as frequency signals, not as a partition.

### 2.1 The Philosophy Behind the Questions: What Are They Really Testing?

After analysing all 207 questions one by one, a clear pattern emerges: Cambridge NST interview questions almost never test 'do you know this fact'. They test three things, in descending order of importance:

**First, how you confront a problem you do not recognise.** This is the single most important assessment dimension in the interview. The interviewer will deliberately present a scenario beyond the A-Level syllabus — the gravitational acceleration on an unknown planet, a reaction mechanism you have never seen, an integral that appears intractable — and then observe your response. Do you freeze, or do you start dismantling it? Do you guess wildly, or do you proceed stepwise from known principles?

**Second, whether you can make progress when given a hint.** An interview is not an examination. Interviewers do not expect you to solve every problem unaided. What they expect is this: when you get stuck, and they offer a hint, can you take it and carry on? This maps directly onto the supervision model — each week the supervisor gives you a direction, and you go away and walk the rest of the path yourself.

**Third, whether your physical or chemical intuition is sound.** Across the 207 questions, a great many require no precise calculation. What they require is: are the dimensions correct? Is the order of magnitude plausible? Is the trend upward or downward? What happens in the limiting cases (t → 0, t → ∞, m → 0, m → ∞)? This kind of intuition is harder to train than computational skill, and it distinguishes 'genuine understanding' from 'can apply the formula' far more effectively.

### 2.2 Distribution of High-Frequency Topics

The following are the most frequently tested topics, based on thematic tagging of all 207 questions:

**Physics (84 questions)**:
- Mechanics (approximately 30 questions) — the absolute core. Covers Newton's laws, tension, collisions, energy conservation, simple harmonic motion, gravitation, and orbits. Mechanics questions appear so often because they provide the most natural setting for reasoning from first principles.
- Graph sketching (approximately 9 questions) — appears in virtually every interview. Not 'sketch y = x²', but 'sketch how this physical quantity varies with that one, and explain every feature'.
- Electromagnetism (approximately 6 questions) — circuit analysis, resistor networks, electric and magnetic field concepts.
- Dimensional analysis and Fermi estimation (approximately 7 questions) — classic vehicles for testing physical intuition.

**Chemistry (60 questions)**:
- Organic reaction mechanisms (approximately 8 questions) — SN1/SN2, esterification, Grignard reactions. The emphasis is not on recall but on explaining 'why' using curly arrows.
- Kinetics (approximately 8 questions) — rate equations, activation energy, catalysts, reaction coordinate diagrams.
- Acid–base equilibria (approximately 8 questions) — pH calculations, buffer solutions, the temperature dependence of K_w.
- Graph sketching (approximately 5 questions) — rate–time graphs, titration curves, Boltzmann distributions.
- Chemical bonding and periodicity (approximately 7 questions) — O–H versus O–D bond strength, the deeper reasons behind periodic trends.

**NST Mathematics (63 questions)**:
- Graph sketching (approximately 17 questions) — the single most frequent question type. From arcsin(cos x) to x^x, from rational functions to parametric equations. Interviewers expect you to discuss domain, asymptotes, symmetry, points of inflection, and end behaviour.
- Calculus (approximately 14 questions) — differentiation, integration, differential equations. The emphasis is not on mechanical computation but on recognising structure (nested chain rules, the choice of u and dv in integration by parts).
- Applied mathematics (approximately 15 questions) — mathematical modelling in mechanics, thermodynamics, and electromagnetism. This is what makes NST mathematics interviews distinctive: mathematics is not abstract but a tool for solving physical and chemical problems.
- Fermi estimation (approximately 6 questions) — from 'how many times must you fold a piece of paper to reach the Moon?' to 'what is the total mass of the Earth's atmosphere?'.

### 2.3 Difficulty Tiers and Characteristic Features

The 207 questions do not sit at a single uniform difficulty. They fall roughly into three tiers (the percentages are indicative, assigned by judgement rather than by any published rubric, and they apply to the corpus as a whole rather than to each subject separately):

**Foundation tier (approximately 30%)** — directly tests depth of understanding of core A-Level concepts. Examples: 'What does F = ma mean? Which of Newton's laws does it represent?' 'At the molecular level, what is the difference between an endothermic and an exothermic reaction?' 'Differentiate tan x.' These questions appear straightforward, but interviewers expect far more than the standard answer. Take F = ma: the interviewer expects you to point out that this is the special case of the second law when mass is constant — the full form is F = dp/dt — and to discuss variable-mass systems (such as a rocket) where ma no longer applies.

**Intermediate tier (approximately 50%)** — applies A-Level concepts to unfamiliar contexts. Examples: 'A stranded astronaut lands on an unknown planet. On Earth she takes 1.0 seconds to jump and land; on this planet she takes 1.4 seconds. Determine the planet's gravitational acceleration.' 'For a reaction with ΔH = −50 kJ/mol and ΔS = −100 J/mol·K, at what temperature does it become non-spontaneous?' 'Differentiate arcsin(cos x).' The core challenge in these questions is: can you recognise a familiar principle hiding inside an unfamiliar shell?

**Advanced tier (approximately 20%)** — demands creative thinking or interdisciplinary synthesis. Examples: 'How would you make a metal oxide that is both electrically conductive and transparent?' 'A cubic resistor network: find the equivalent resistance between opposite vertices.' 'Estimate the total mass of the Earth's atmosphere.' These questions have no standard solution method. The interviewer wants to see you build a model, make reasonable assumptions, and check dimensions and orders of magnitude — the entire process matters far more than the final number.

### 2.4 Cross-Disciplinary Intersections: The Unique DNA of NST Interviews

The most striking feature of NST interviews is the deep interweaving of physics, chemistry, and mathematics. Among the 207 questions, at least 30 explicitly involve cross-disciplinary reasoning:

- **Physics → Chemistry**: using thermodynamics to explain chemical equilibrium, quantum mechanics to explain chemical bonding, electromagnetism to explain intermolecular forces.
- **Chemistry → Physics**: using reaction kinetics to explain radioactive decay, electrochemistry to explain how batteries work.
- **Mathematics → Physics/Chemistry**: using calculus to derive equations of motion, differential equations to model reaction rates, probability theory to understand entropy.

This cross-pollination is not accidental — it lies at the heart of the NST curriculum design. What interviewers are looking for is not a student who is 'good at physics' or 'good at chemistry', but one who can understand chemistry through a physical lens and wield mathematical tools to solve scientific problems.

### 2.5 Preparation Strategy: Where to Begin

Based on the analysis above, here are preparation recommendations in priority order:

**First priority: train yourself to think aloud.** This is the single most important skill. Find a classmate or a teacher, take a question you have never seen, and talk through it as you work. Record yourself. Play it back. Check: is your reasoning coherent? Do you articulate your assumptions? When you get stuck, do you fall silent or do you try to describe where the blockage lies? Twenty minutes a day, and after two weeks the improvement will be qualitative.

**Second priority: graph-sketching fluency.** Across all 207 questions, graph sketching is the highest-frequency question type spanning physics, chemistry, and mathematics alike. How to practise: take any function or physical relationship and, before drawing the precise graph, ask yourself — what is the domain? Are there asymptotes? Where does it cross the axes? Is the trend rising or falling? Are there turning points? What is the end behaviour (x → 0, x → ∞)? Then sketch quickly and check against the accurate plot. Appendices A, B, and C mark every graph question with a full guided walkthrough.

**Third priority: derivation from first principles.** Do not memorise formulae; re-derive them from fundamental principles each time. For example: do not memorise 'tension in a lift cable T = m(g+a)'. Instead, start from Newton's second law, T − mg = ma, every single time. This habit is critical in the interview — when you face an unfamiliar context, the only thing you can rely on is first principles. Every question in the appendices models this approach.

**Fourth priority: Fermi estimation and dimensional analysis.** Practise estimating the orders of magnitude of everyday quantities: the number of air molecules in a classroom, the number of grains of sand on Earth, the diameter of a human hair. The key is to develop 'order-of-magnitude intuition' — is the answer in the range of 10³, 10⁶, or 10⁹? At the same time, cultivate the habit of dimensional checking: do both sides of every equation have consistent dimensions?

**A three-second discipline that catches most slip-ups.** Before you state any numerical answer, run three checks in this order:

1. **Dimensions** — do the units on both sides agree?
2. **Units within the calculation** — have you mixed kJ with J, or cm³ with m³? The most common single error in the questions below is a kJ/J slip (see Q1 in Appendix B, where ΔS is quoted in J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹ while ΔH is in kJ mol⁻¹).
3. **Magnitude** — is the answer physically plausible? If you have just computed that a ship bobs with an amplitude of 4.7 m (Appendix A §1.3.10) or that a point charge is 3.0 C (Appendix A §2.2.1), say so: the method may be right while the data are not.

Apply the same discipline to **convention-dependent quantities**: state whether you are counting stereoisomers, whether "5 colours" permits repetition, and whether a capacitor is isolated or connected, *before* you give a number. Half the "wrong answers" in interview questions are unstated conventions, not errors.

**Fifth priority: know your interviewers.** Read the supervisor profiles in Chapter 3 of this report and the paper analyses in Appendix D. Understanding your interviewers' research interests is not about 'predicting questions'. It is about understanding their intellectual style and academic taste. A mechanics problem posed by an astrophysicist and a structure question posed by a solid-state chemist draw on different underlying frameworks of thought.

### 2.6 On the Day: Mindset and Pacing

Finally, some practical advice on pacing for the interview day itself:

**Preparation time (unique to Pembroke)**: 30 to 40 minutes of independent problem-solving. Do not try to finish every problem — pick the two or three you are most confident about, work through them in depth, and leave a clear written trail of reasoning. The interviewers will look at your rough work.

**During the interview**: if you get stuck, say so. Say 'I notice there's a contradiction here', 'I'm wondering whether we could use energy conservation', 'these dimensions don't look right'. Any of these is a hundred times better than silence. The interviewers need to see your thought process, not just the final answer.

**When you receive a hint**: pause, listen carefully, then say 'let me think about that…' before continuing. Do not let nerves cause you to ignore the hint and press on down your own wrong path. When interviewers give a hint, they are steering you — take it.

**At the end**: if the interviewer asks 'do you have any questions?', this is an opportunity to demonstrate curiosity. Ask something about their research, or about the Cambridge NST course — it shows you have done your homework and genuinely care.

## Chapter 3: Pembroke Physical and Chemistry Interview Supervisors

[[#Contents]]

At Cambridge, the undergraduate interview is, at its core, a 'teaching match' — the interviewers are often the very supervisors who will teach you in the years ahead. Understanding their backgrounds, research interests, and teaching styles is not about predicting questions; it is about understanding the angle from which they are likely to ask and the intellectual qualities they value.

### 3.1 Physical Sciences

**Professor Mark Wyatt** is the figure most likely to appear in a Pembroke physical sciences interview. As Director of Studies for Part IA Physical NST, he also oversees Part II/III teaching in astrophysics. Professor Wyatt holds a position at Cambridge's Institute of Astronomy. (Some sources describe him as co-Director of the Institute; this could not be confirmed against the Institute's leadership pages, so treat the specific role as unconfirmed and do not repeat it as fact.) His research focuses on the formation and evolution of planetary systems, and he is particularly known for his work on debris disks and the indirect detection of exoplanets. His academic impact is substantial — an h-index of approximately 68 with over 14,000 citations (Google Scholar, as of 2026; citation metrics are time-sensitive and should be re-checked before quoting). His recent work includes participation in the ALMA ARKS survey, delivering high-resolution imaging of 24 debris disks, and modelling of the Fomalhaut debris disk using JWST data.

Wyatt's research draws heavily on classical physics concepts — gravitation, orbital mechanics, black-body radiation, the Doppler effect — which happen also to be the core content of A-Level Physics. His interview questions, accordingly, tend to start from fundamental physical concepts and test the applicant's ability to apply them in an astrophysical context. If you encounter a question about orbits or gravity in your interview, it is worth remembering: the physics behind that question is the same physics that Professor Wyatt uses in his research every day.

**Dr F. Lee** is the other Director of Studies for the physical sciences. There is a notable information gap in the public record — Pembroke's official list gives only 'Dr F. Lee (Physics)', and online searches have not been able to confirm the full name or research field. Applicants are advised to consult the Pembroke Fellows directory or the Cavendish Laboratory personnel directory directly for the most up-to-date information.

### 3.2 Chemistry

**Dr Stephanie Smith** is Pembroke's Director of Studies for Chemistry, and the examiner most likely to conduct your chemistry interview. Her professional identity is that of a Teaching Fellow whose core remit is pedagogy — a fact that carries important implications for interview preparation strategy. Dr Smith completed her undergraduate degree (Selwyn College, Natural Sciences — Chemistry), master's, and doctorate at Cambridge, with her doctoral research in molecular modelling. She currently teaches IA Chemistry, IB Chemistry A (Physical Chemistry), IB Chemistry B (Organic/Inorganic), and selected Part II courses — covering virtually the entire core chemistry curriculum from first to third year.

In 2023, she received the Pilkington Teaching Prize — one of Cambridge's highest teaching honours — having previously won two student-led teaching awards. Professor James Keeler, Head of the Department of Chemistry, is reported to have described her as the only member of the department who can move seamlessly from synthetic organic chemistry to high-resolution spectroscopy to theoretical techniques in her teaching. *(This quotation appears in teaching-award coverage; no dated primary source was located during verification, so treat the wording as reported rather than verbatim.)* **Unverified:** a Newnham College affiliation is sometimes reported for Dr Smith but could not be confirmed against any college or University directory; she is documented as a Pembroke Teaching Fellow. Do not rely on this detail.

Because Dr Smith is a teaching-focused supervisor, her interview is more likely to centre on depth of understanding of fundamental chemical concepts and clarity of scientific expression, rather than on probing knowledge of frontier research. When preparing for her interview, the emphasis should fall on 'can I explain a chemical concept clearly and precisely?' rather than 'how much do I know about the latest research developments?'.

**Professor Dame Clare Grey**, although not listed as a DoS, is Pembroke's most senior Fellow in chemistry and exerts an undeniable influence on the college's chemistry admissions. Professor Grey holds the Geoffrey Moorhouse Gibson Chair of Chemistry at Cambridge, is a Fellow of the Royal Society (FRS), and was appointed Dame Commander of the Order of the British Empire (DBE) in 2022. Her research spans solid-state NMR spectroscopy and battery materials; with an h-index of approximately 138 and over 74,000 citations (Google Scholar, as of 2026), she ranks among the world's foremost scholars in energy-storage materials. She is also a co-founder of the battery-technology company Nyobolt.

For applicants in physical chemistry or materials science, Professor Grey's research — using NMR to study lithium dynamics inside batteries during charge and discharge, in situ — represents an exceptionally valuable area to explore. Appendix D includes detailed analyses of two of her representative papers.

# Appendix A: Cambridge Physics Interview Questions — Interactive Guided Exploration

[[#Contents]]

> **How to Use This Document**
>
> This document contains 84 Cambridge Natural Sciences (Physical) undergraduate interview questions, each redesigned according to the **Socratic guided-teaching method of Cambridge supervisions**.
>
> Each question is broken down into multiple steps, with each step hidden inside a clickable collapsible block. This is not an "answer book" — it is a **simulated interview experience**:
> - See the question first; think for yourself
> - Click Step 1 to see what hint the interviewer might give you
> - Click the next step to advance your reasoning gradually
> - Only the final step reveals the complete answer
> - Where a question carries a **✨ A Sharper Route** block, that is a genuinely more elegant method than the one walked through above — read it *after* you have finished the question, and ask yourself why it is shorter
>
> Cambridge interviewers (dons) will not tell you the answer directly. They ask questions, offer hints, and let you discover for yourself. This document attempts to recreate that experience of "guided discovery".
>
> **Format notes**:
> - All mathematical formulae use Unicode plain-text symbols (Greek letters, superscripts, subscripts, fractions, etc.)
> - Click each collapsible block to expand and read its contents
> - Suggested approach: read the question → think for yourself → then expand the steps and compare with your reasoning
>
> ---

## 1. Mechanics

### 1.1 Kinematics & Newton's Laws

---

#### 1.1.1 What does F = ma mean?

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer poses the problem</b></summary>

**Problem**: What does F = ma mean? Which of Newton's laws is this? Explain in terms of inertia.

The interviewer expects you to pause and think first — the question looks simple, but the trap lies in "which of Newton's laws is this?" Do not rush to answer.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — Is this the full second law?</b></summary>

The interviewer may press you: "Are you sure F = ma is the **complete form** of the second law?"

Think about it: what was Newton's original statement? He did not say F = ma — he said the **rate of change of momentum equals the net external force**:

**F** = (d**p**)/(dt)

When the mass m is constant, (d**p**)/(dt) = (d(m**v**))/(dt) = m(d**v**)/(dt) = m**a**.

So F = ma is only a **special case when the mass is constant**. Can you think of a physical situation where the mass is not constant?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Going deeper — unpack each symbol one by one</b></summary>

The interviewer may now ask you to explain the physical meaning of each symbol individually:

- **F**: the **net external force** acting on the body, a vector. Note the word "net" — if several forces act simultaneously, you must do a **vector sum** first. The interviewer might ask: "If I push a box across a table, is F the force of my hand?" — no, F is the thrust **minus friction**, i.e. the net force.

- **m**: **inertial mass** — a measure of the body's resistance to changes in its state of motion. This is a scalar.

- **a**: **acceleration**, a vector, in the same direction as the net external force.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🎯 Step 4: Synthesis — deeper connection to inertia</b></summary>

The interviewer now expects you to tie all the threads together:

**Inertia** is the tendency of a body to maintain its existing state of motion (the essence of Newton's first law). F = ma **quantifies** this qualitative concept:

- To change the state of motion of a body possessing inertia (to produce acceleration), **an external force must be applied**
- Greater force → greater acceleration (proportional relationship)
- Greater mass (inertia) → smaller acceleration for the same force (inverse relationship)

**Mass is the measure of inertia**. F = ma turns the philosophical concept of "inertia" into a computable, quantitative relationship.

**Extension discussion** (if the interviewer keeps probing):
- In **special relativity**, rest mass m₀ is invariant; momentum is p = γm₀v where γ = 1/√(1−v²/c²). The correct relativistic equation of motion is **F** = d**p**/dt = d(γm₀**v**)/dt, not F = m_relativistic × a. The outdated concept of 'relativistic mass increasing with speed' has been largely abandoned in modern physics.
- **Variable-mass systems** (e.g. a rocket ejecting fuel): write the thrust with one consistent convention. If u_rel is the exhaust velocity **relative to the rocket** (a vector pointing backwards), the equation of motion is

  m(dv/dt) = F_ext + u_rel (dm/dt),   with dm/dt less than 0 during burn,

  so the thrust term −u_rel|dm/dt| points forwards. (If you prefer to work with the positive exhaust *speed* w, write m(dv/dt) = F_ext − w(dm/dt) and remember w is measured backwards along the rocket axis.) The compact form F = d(mv)/dt holds only when the mass collection is clearly defined and no mass crosses the system boundary relative to the centre of mass — which is exactly why the rocket needs the extra term.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

F = ma is the **special form of Newton's second law when the mass is constant**. It expresses quantitatively the relationship between net external force, inertial mass, and acceleration: net force equals inertial mass multiplied by acceleration. The complete form is **F** = d**p**/dt.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"What are the dimensions of F = dp/dt, and does the equation retain its form in every inertial frame?" Write [F] = MLT⁻² and check that dp/dt has the same dimensions: [p] = MLT⁻¹, so [dp/dt] = MLT⁻². Then ask: "If two observers move at constant relative velocity, does each measure the same force?" Because acceleration is invariant under Galilean boosts, yes — so F = ma holds in every inertial frame, whereas F = mv (momentum itself) would not distinguish rest from uniform motion. This route stresses that the second law is a statement about change in momentum, not momentum alone, and it links directly to the first law's special case (F = 0 ⇒ p constant).

</details>

---

#### 1.1.2 Hanging mass in a lift — find the tension in the rope

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A box of mass 5 kg hangs by a light inextensible rope in an elevator. The lift accelerates upwards at 4 m/s². What is the tension in the rope?

Don't rush to plug numbers into a formula. The interviewer wants to see **how you approach the analysis** — where do you start?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — draw a force diagram</b></summary>

The interviewer may push the paper towards you: "Draw it."

Two forces act on the box:
- **Weight** mg, directed vertically downwards
- **Tension** T in the rope, directed vertically upwards

The rope is "light and inextensible" — this means the rope's mass is negligible, and the tension is uniform throughout.

Now set up a coordinate system. Which direction do you take as positive?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Write the equation and solve</b></summary>

Take **upwards as the positive direction**. The box accelerates upwards with the lift at a = 4 m/s².

Newton's second law (net force = mass × acceleration):

T - mg = ma

T = m(g + a) = 5 × (9.81 + 4) = 5 × 13.81 = 69.05 N

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Boundary check — is your answer reasonable?</b></summary>

The interviewer may ask: "How do you know your answer is right? Test the extreme cases."

- If a = 0 (stationary or constant velocity): T = mg = 49 N ✓ (the rope merely supports the weight)
- If a = -g (free fall, rope slack): T = m(g-g) = 0 ✓
- If a > 0 (accelerating upwards): T > mg ✓ (we feel "heavier", which makes sense)

Physical intuition: when the lift accelerates upwards, you feel heavier — the rope also "feels" the box is heavier.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Tension in the rope T = 69.1 N (3 s.f.; 69.05 N before rounding).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✨ A Sharper Route — change the frame</b></summary>

**Why it is worth knowing:** the lab-frame route works, but you have to track the sign of `a` by hand every time. Changing the frame removes the sign problem entirely — and it generalises to every "apparent weight" question you will ever be asked.

**Key insight:** an upward acceleration `a` is indistinguishable from an extra uniform gravitational field. Work in the lift frame and this becomes a statics problem.

**Steps:**

1. In the lift frame the box is at rest, so forces balance against a downward pseudo-force `ma`.
2. The effective weight is therefore `m(g + a)`, and the rope must simply support it.
3. Hence `T = m(g + a) = 5 × 13.81 = 69.05 N`.
4. Check the two extremes: `a = 0` gives `T = mg`; free fall (`a = −g`) gives `T = 0` — the box floats, exactly as it should.

**Result:** `T = m(g + a)`.

**Why it is better:** one physical equivalence replaces a sign convention. The same idea answers every follow-up — a stack of boxes, a spring balance, a tilted lift, a lift accelerating downwards — because the answer is always just "the apparent weight in `g_eff`.

</details>

---

#### 1.1.3 Hang a second box below the first — tension in the new rope

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer extends the problem</b></summary>

**Problem**: Now connect another box via a light inextensible rope to the bottom of the first box. What is the tension in this new rope? (Hint: equivalence principle)

The interviewer has deliberately given a hint — "equivalence principle". What is this hint telling you? Think about Einstein's lift thought experiment…

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — the elegance of the equivalence principle</b></summary>

The interviewer's hint is an elegant shortcut.

In an upward-accelerating lift, an observer inside experiences a downward **pseudo-force** (in the non-inertial frame of the lift). This is indistinguishable from a stronger gravitational field, so for an observer in the lift the **effective gravitational acceleration** is:

g_eff = g + a = 9.81 + 4 = 13.81 m/s²

In this "effective gravitational field", everything behaves as if gravity were stronger. The lower rope supports only the second box — so the tension is simply the second box's "effective weight".

But wait — what is the mass of the second box? The original question doesn't state it explicitly!

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Answer parametrically — do not invent a mass</b></summary>

The second box's mass is **not given** in the problem. In an interview, the correct move is to **refuse to plug in a number** and instead give the answer in terms of the (unknown) masses m₁ and m₂. This is a test of whether you will silently invent data for an ill-posed problem.

In the effective gravitational field g_eff = g + a, the lower rope supports only the second box, so its tension is the second box's "effective weight":

T_bottom = m₂ × g_eff = m₂(g + a)

The upper rope supports **both** boxes (everything below it), so:

T_top = (m₁ + m₂) × g_eff = (m₁ + m₂)(g + a)

This is the complete, correct answer. Notice the pattern: *each rope supports the weight of everything below it, with every element accelerated upward at a* — a "cut-and-isolate" argument. If you cut the upper rope, the whole lower subsystem (mass m₁ + m₂) must be accelerated upward at a, so T_top = (m₁ + m₂)(g + a). This is the generalisable insight interviewers reward.

*Only if the interviewer then tells you the masses* (e.g. "both are 5 kg") do you substitute: T_bottom = 5 × 13.81 = 69.05 N, T_top = 10 × 13.81 = 138.1 N.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Tension in the lower rope = m₂(g + a); tension in the upper rope = (m₁ + m₂)(g + a). (If both boxes are 5 kg, these are 69 N and 138 N respectively.)

</details>

---

#### 1.1.4 Lift braking to a stop — find the cable tension

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes a new scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A lift of mass 500 kg travels downwards at 5.0 m/s. It is brought to rest by constant acceleration over 6.0 m. What is the tension in the cable? Then repeat for upward motion.

This question has two parts — braking from downward motion and braking from upward motion. Are they symmetric? Don't rush to calculate.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — find the acceleration first</b></summary>

The interviewer: "What do you need to know first before you can find the tension?"

Known quantities: m = 500 kg, u = 5.0 m/s (downwards), v = 0, s = 6.0 m.

Use the kinematic equation that doesn't involve t:

v² = u² + 2as ⇒ 0 = 5.0² + 2a(6.0)

a = -(25/12) = -2.083 m/s²

What does the negative sign mean? In the equation v² = u² + 2as we took the *downward* direction as positive (u = +5.0 m/s downward). The negative a therefore means the acceleration is **opposite** to the initial velocity, i.e. **upwards**. The lift is moving downwards but decelerating, so the acceleration points upwards.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Force analysis — braking from downward motion</b></summary>

Take **upwards as the positive direction**. The lift experiences weight mg (downwards, i.e. -mg) and cable tension T (upwards).

T - mg = ma

T = m(g + a) = 500 × (9.81 + 2.083) = 500 × 11.893 = 5947 N ≈ 5.95 kN

Note: although the lift is moving downwards, it is decelerating — the acceleration is upwards — so the tension is **greater than** the weight. You feel "heavier".

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 4: Braking from upward motion</b></summary>

The lift moves upwards (initial velocity upwards); decelerating means the acceleration is **downwards**.

Taking upwards as positive, a = -2.083 m/s²:

T = m(g + a) = 500 × (9.81 - 2.083) = 500 × 7.727 = 3864 N ≈ 3.86 kN

The tension is **less than** the weight — you feel "lighter". This matches the sensation when a lift approaches the top floor.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 5: Physical intuition check</b></summary>

- Braking from downward motion: T = 5.95 kN > mg = 4.90 kN (extra upward force needed to decelerate) ✓
- Braking from upward motion: T = 3.86 kN ⋖ mg = 4.90 kN (gravity helps the deceleration) ✓

The magnitude of acceleration is the same in both cases, but the tensions differ — because the direction of gravity is unchanged whilst the acceleration direction changes.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Braking from downward motion: T ≈ 5.95 kN; braking from upward motion: T ≈ 3.86 kN.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"Can you find the tension without computing the acceleration first?" Use the work–energy theorem over the 6.0 m stop. Take upwards as positive: the lift's displacement is s = −6.0 m, and ΔK = 0 − ½(500)(5.0)² = −6250 J. The net work is (T − mg)s, so (T − mg)(−6.0) = −6250 ⇒ T − mg = +1042 N ⇒ T = 5947 N. The sign trap is real: velocity and displacement must carry the same convention, and here both point downwards. This route is worth mastering because it replaces two kinematics steps with a single energy statement, and it generalises at once to any stopping distance or a non-constant braking force.

</details>

---

#### 1.1.5 Car braking distance

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A car travels at constant speed u = 7.00 m/s. The driver sees an amber light and brakes. Total resistive force = 1/6 of weight. How far does the car travel during braking?

The interviewer has given the relationship between resistive force and weight — this is a key clue.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — from force to acceleration</b></summary>

Resistive force F = ⅙mg. From Newton's second law:

F = ma ⇒ ⅙mg = ma ⇒ a = (g/6)

The magnitude of deceleration is g/6 ≈ 1.635 m/s². Note: m cancels out — the braking deceleration is independent of the car's mass! Why? (Because both the resistive force and inertial mass are proportional to m.)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Kinematic solution for distance</b></summary>

v² = u² - 2as (a taken as positive, direction opposite to motion)

0 = 7.00² - 2 × (g/6) × s

s = (u²)/(2 × g/6) = (3u²)/(g) = (3 × 49)/(9.81) = 14.98 m ≈ 15.0 m

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Dimensional check and extension discussion</b></summary>

Dimensional check: [u²/g] = [L²/T²] / [L/T²] = [L] ✓

The interviewer may follow up: "What if the speed doubles?"

s ∝ u² — doubling the speed to 14 m/s makes the braking distance 4 times as long, about 60 m. This is why speed limits are critical for safety.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Braking distance s ≈ 15.0 m.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"Why find the acceleration at all?" The resistive force does work equal to the loss of kinetic energy: F·s = ½mu², with F = ⅙mg. The mass cancels immediately: ⅙mg·s = ½mu² ⇒ s = 3u²/g. This is one line instead of two steps, and it makes transparent why the answer is independent of the car's mass — both the retarding force and the inertia scale with m. It also generalises at once to a non-constant resistive force: if F(v) is given, s = ∫½mu²/F(v) becomes the natural extension, whereas the kinematic route would require integrating a(v).

</details>

---

#### 1.1.6 Bullet embedded in a block — kinetic energy loss

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the collision scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A bullet of mass m moving horizontally at speed u meets a block of wood of mass M travelling in the opposite direction at speed U, and embeds in it. Show the loss of kinetic energy.

"Embeds" — this word tells you the type of collision. What is conserved? What is not?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — perfectly inelastic collision</b></summary>

The bullet embeds itself in the block — this is a **perfectly inelastic collision**. In such a collision:
- **Momentum is conserved** (no external force on the system, or the collision time is so short that external impulse is negligible)
- **Kinetic energy is not conserved** (some kinetic energy is converted to internal energy — heat, deformation)

First, take the bullet's initial direction of motion as the positive direction.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Conservation of momentum — find the final velocity</b></summary>

Initial total momentum: pᵢ = mu - MU

After the collision the two move with a common velocity V:

(m + M)V = mu - MU ⇒ V = (mu - MU)/(m + M)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 4: Calculate the kinetic energy loss</b></summary>

Initial kinetic energy: KEᵢ = ½mu² + ½MU²

Final kinetic energy: KE_f = ½(m+M)V² = ((mu - MU)²)/(2(m+M))

Kinetic energy loss Δ KE = KEᵢ - KE_f. After algebraic simplification:

Δ KE = (mM(u + U)²)/(2(m + M))

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 5: Interpreting the physical significance</b></summary>

Notice the structure of the Δ KE expression:
- Δ KE ∝ (u+U)² — the larger the **relative speed**, the greater the loss (a head-on collision loses more energy than a rear-end collision)
- Δ KE = ½μ(u+U)², where μ = mM/(m+M) is the **reduced mass** — the energy loss is exactly the kinetic energy of the *relative motion* (approach speed u+U) evaluated with the reduced mass
- ΔKE > 0 unless the relative speed is zero. (This is a consequence of the collision being perfectly inelastic — there is no need to invoke the second law of thermodynamics, and doing so is rhetoric rather than justification.)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Kinetic energy loss Δ KE = (mM(u + U)²)/(2(m + M)). The lost energy is converted to internal energy (heat and deformation).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"Which frame makes the energy loss transparent?" Transform to the centre-of-mass frame, where the total momentum is zero and the two bodies approach each other with relative speed u+U. In that frame the collision is symmetric: the available kinetic energy is entirely 'relative', namely ½μ(u+U)² with μ = mM/(m+M). Since the collision is perfectly inelastic, all of that relative kinetic energy disappears in the CM frame — and relative kinetic energy is frame-invariant. Hence ΔKE = ½μ(u+U)² = mM(u+U)²/(2(m+M)). This route shows why the answer depends only on the relative speed, not on the arbitrary lab-frame split between u and U.

</details>

---

#### 1.1.7 The lost astronaut — gravitational acceleration on an unknown planet

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A lost astronaut lands on an unknown planet. On Earth (g = 9.81 m/s²) she takes 1.0 s to jump up and land. On this planet, a jump takes 1.4 s. What is g on the strange planet? (Assume same jump speed.)

What is the key assumption? "Same jump speed" — what does that imply?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — model the jump</b></summary>

The astronaut jumps vertically upwards with initial speed v₀ and then lands back on the ground. The ascent and descent times are equal (by symmetry, neglecting air resistance).

Total flight time: T = (2v₀)/(g)

The key assumption here is: **v₀ is the same on both planets** — her leg muscle power is unchanged, so the initial speed imparted by pushing off the ground is the same. This is a reasonable biomechanical assumption.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Solve using ratios</b></summary>

On Earth: T_E = (2v₀)/(g_E) = 1.0 s

On the unknown planet: T_P = (2v₀)/(g_P) = 1.4 s

Dividing the two equations (v₀ cancels):

(T_P)/(T_E) = (g_E)/(g_P) ⇒ g_P = g_E × (T_E)/(T_P) = 9.81 × (1.0/1.4) = 7.01 m/s²

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Intuition check</b></summary>

g_P = 7.0 m/s² ⋖ 9.81 m/s². On a planet with weaker gravity, the jump takes longer — both ascent and descent are slower. This fits intuition: astronauts' jumps on the Moon do look more "floaty".

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

g on the unknown planet ≈ 7.0 m/s².

</details>

---

#### 1.1.8 The bolt thrower — maximum range problem

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A bolt thrower fires a bolt horizontally at speed v. The bolt must make an angle of at least θ = 45° to the horizontal when it hits the ground. Find the maximum range from height h = 10 m, and the speed required.

Note: "fired horizontally" but "angle at impact at least 45°" — what constraint links these two conditions?

**⚠️ First, flag the ambiguity.** This problem is genuinely ambiguous, and a strong candidate should say so before calculating. There are two distinct readings:

1. **"For a given speed v, find the range, subject to θ ≥ 45°."** Here v is fixed and the range is simply x = v√(2h/g), valid only for v ≤ √(2gh).
2. **"What is the largest range achievable at all, and what speed achieves it?"** Here we *optimise over v* subject to the θ ≥ 45° constraint.

The solution below adopts reading (2) — the "maximum range" and "the speed required" (singular) phrasing points that way. But you should state your interpretation explicitly and be ready to switch if the interviewer meant reading (1).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — set up the equations of motion</b></summary>

The bolt is fired horizontally from height h = 10 m (initial vertical velocity is zero).

Horizontal (uniform motion): x = vt

Vertical (free fall): y = h - ½gt²

At impact y = 0: t_land = √(2h/g)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Velocity components at impact and the angle condition</b></summary>

At impact:
- Horizontal velocity: vₓ = v (unchanged, no horizontal force)
- Vertical velocity: v_y = -gt_land = -√2gh

Impact angle θ satisfies: tanθ = (|v_y|)/(vₓ) = (√2gh)/(v)

Requirement θ ≥ 45°: tanθ ≥ 1 ⇒ (√2gh)/(v) ≥ 1 ⇒ v ≤ √2gh

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🎯 Step 4: Maximum range</b></summary>

Range x = v · t_land = v√(2h/g). To maximise the range, v must be as large as possible — but v is constrained by the angle condition v ≤ √2gh.

Take the maximum allowed speed v_max = √2gh:

xₘₐₓ = √2gh × √((2h/g)) = 2h = 20 m

A beautifully simple result: the maximum range equals exactly 2h!

Numerically: v_max = √(2 × 9.81 × 10) = √(196.2) ≈ 14.0 m/s

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Required initial speed v ≈ 14.0 m/s; maximum range = 20 m (exactly 2h, when the impact angle is exactly 45°).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"Plot the velocity vector at impact." The bolt is launched horizontally, so its trajectory in velocity space is a vertical straight line: v_x is fixed at v, while v_y runs from 0 down to −√(2gh). The impact-angle condition θ ≥ 45° means the final velocity vector makes at least 45° with the horizontal, i.e. |v_y| ≥ v_x. Since |v_y| is fixed at √(2gh) by the 10 m drop, the condition is simply v ≤ √(2gh). The range is then v·t_fall = v√(2h/g), maximised at the endpoint v = √(2gh), giving x_max = 2h. This picture makes the constraint a matter of comparing two fixed vertical components rather than solving an inequality.

</details>

---

#### 1.1.9 Cannon targeting behind a wall

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A cannon on horizontal ground targets a point T, 25 m behind a narrow wall. The cannon fires only at 45° and one speed u = 35 m/s. At what distance d must the cannon be placed? The wall is 15 m high — does the cannonball go over?

Two sub-problems here: range positioning + wall clearance check. Which do you tackle first?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — find the total range first</b></summary>

Range for a projectile fired at 45° elevation with initial speed u = 35 m/s:

R = (u²sin(2θ))/(g) = (35² × sin(90°))/(9.81) = (1225/9.81) ≈ 124.9 m

Target T is 25 m behind the wall. If the cannon is at x=0, the wall is at x=d, and the target is at x = d + 25 = R.

So d = R − 25 = 124.9 − 25 ≈ 99.9 m — call it 1.0 × 10² m (distance from cannon to wall). Keep the unrounded value if you go on to evaluate the trajectory height, because the inputs are two-digit and rounding here propagates.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Trajectory equation — check wall clearance</b></summary>

Trajectory equation for a projectile fired at 45°:

y = xtan(45°) - (gx²)/(2u²cos²(45°)) = x - (9.81x²)/(2 × 1225 × 0.5) = x - 0.008008x²

At the wall position x = d = 100 m:

y(100) = 100 - 0.008008 × 10000 = 100 - 80.08 = 19.92 m > 15 m

It clears!

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Maximum height — extra confirmation</b></summary>

Maximum height occurs at x = R/2 = 62.45 m:

yₘₐₓ = 62.45 - 0.008008 × 3900 = 62.45 - 31.23 = 31.2 m

The wall height of 15 m is well below the maximum height of 31.2 m; unless the wall is right next to the cannon or the target, the cannonball will clear it.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

The cannon should be placed about 1.0 × 10² m from the wall (d = 99.9 m unrounded); the cannonball's height at the wall is about 20 m > 15 m, so it clears. (Quoting 19.9 m overstates the precision available from two-digit inputs.)

</details>

---

#### 1.1.10 The bouncing ball — total distance and total time

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A ball is dropped from rest at height h₀ and bounces such that hₙ = α hₙ₋₁ (α = 0.25, h₀ = 3.0 m). How far does the ball travel before coming to rest? How long does it take?

Each bounce height is 1/4 of the previous one — this is a geometric series. But note: each bounce involves both an ascent **and** a descent (except the first drop, which is descent only).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — series summation for total distance</b></summary>

- First drop: h₀ = 3.0 m
- First bounce up: h₁ = α h₀ = 0.75 m, then falls h₁
- Second bounce up: h₂ = α² h₀, then falls h₂
- ...

Total distance:

S = h₀ + 2h₁ + 2h₂ + 2h₃ + … = h₀ + 2h₀(α + α² + α³ + …)

S = h₀ + 2h₀ · (α/1-α) = h₀(1 + (2α/1-α)) = h₀(1+α)/(1-α)

S = 3.0 × (1.25/0.75) = 3.0 × (5/3) = 5.0 m

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Series summation for total time</b></summary>

Time for each drop: t_down,n = √(2hₙ/g) = √(2αⁿ h₀/g)

Ascent time = descent time (by symmetry).

T = t₀ + 2t₁ + 2t₂ + … = √((2h₀)/(g))[1 + 2√α + 2(√α)² + 2(√α)³ + …]

T = √((2h₀)/(g))[1 + (2√α)/(1-√α)] = √((2h₀)/(g)) · (1+√α)/(1-√α)

√α = √(0.25) = 0.5:

T = √((2 × 3.0)/(9.81)) × (1.5/0.5) = √(0.6116) × 3 = 0.782 × 3 = 2.35 s

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Philosophical discussion — a flavour of Zeno's paradox</b></summary>

In theory, the ball never "completely" stops — each bounce height is 1/4 of the previous one, subdividing indefinitely. Yet both the total distance and the total time **converge to finite values** — this is the beauty of geometric series summation.

In practice, when the bounce height falls below the thermal motion at the molecular scale, we can consider the ball to have stopped. The convergence of the mathematics guarantees physical reasonableness.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Total distance = 5.0 m; total time ≈ 2.35 s.

</details>

---

#### 1.1.11 Police spaceship — splitting to pursue

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A police spaceship of mass 10,000 kg travelling at 2.00 km/s needs to arrest another ship ahead at 2.50 km/s. It can split into two equal parts with 2.00 GJ of kinetic energy from a reserve. Calculate the speed of the pursuing part. Was the reserve sufficient?

What is conserved during the split? Where does the energy come from?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — conservation laws</b></summary>

- Total mass M = 10,000 kg, initial velocity v₀ = 2000 m/s
- Splits into two equal parts, each m = 5,000 kg
- Reserve energy E_reserve = 2.00 × 10⁹ J

**Momentum is conserved during the split** (internal forces do not change the total momentum):

Mv₀ = mv₁ + mv₂ ⇒ 10,000 × 2000 = 5,000(v₁ + v₂) ⇒ v₁ + v₂ = 4,000 (1)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Energy relation</b></summary>

Total kinetic energy before the split: KEᵢ = ½Mv₀² = ½ × 10,000 × 4,000,000 = 2.00 × 10¹⁰ J

Total kinetic energy after the split = KEᵢ + E_reserve (reserve energy converted to kinetic energy):

½m(v₁² + v₂²) = 2.00 × 10¹⁰ + 2.00 × 10⁹ = 2.20 × 10¹⁰

v₁² + v₂² = 8,800,000 (2)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 4: Solve the system of equations</b></summary>

From (1): v₂ = 4,000 - v₁, substitute into (2):

v₁² + (4,000 - v₁)² = 8,800,000

2v₁² - 8,000v₁ + 7,200,000 = 0

v₁² - 4,000v₁ + 3,600,000 = 0

Discriminant Δ = 16,000,000 - 14,400,000 = 1,600,000

v₁ = (4,000 ± 1,264.9)/(2) = 2,632 m/s or 1,368 m/s

The pursuing part takes the larger value: v₁ = 2,632.5 m/s ≈ 2.63 km/s

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 5: Judge whether it is sufficient</b></summary>

Pursuing part speed 2.63 km/s > 2.50 km/s (target speed) → it can catch up.

The reserve energy of 2.00 GJ is sufficient.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Pursuing part speed ≈ 2.63 km/s > 2.50 km/s; the reserve energy is sufficient.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach: solve in the centre-of-mass frame</b></summary>

The main solution works in the lab frame and solves a quadratic. A cleaner approach is to transform to the **centre-of-mass frame**:

1. The centre of mass moves at v₀ = 2000 m/s (unchanged by the internal split).
2. In the CM frame, the two halves (each m = 5000 kg) fly apart with equal and opposite momenta. Let their CM-frame speeds be ±u.
3. The reserve energy 2.00 GJ is entirely converted into the *relative* kinetic energy in the CM frame: 2 × ½mu² = 2.00 × 10⁹ J ⇒ u² = 2.00 × 10⁹ / 5000 = 4.0 × 10⁵ ⇒ u = 632.5 m/s.
4. Transform back to the lab frame: the pursuing part has speed v₀ + u = 2000 + 632.5 = 2632.5 m/s; the other part has v₀ − u = 1367.5 m/s.

This avoids the quadratic entirely and makes the physics transparent: the reserve energy goes into the *relative* motion, while the centre of mass coasts at v₀. It also directly shows why the two final speeds are symmetric about v₀.

</details>

---


---


---

#### 1.1.12 Rolling vs sliding down a slope — which is faster?

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer springs a classic trap</b></summary>

**Problem**: Which reaches the bottom of a slope faster: a ball rolling down a rough slope, or a ball sliding down a smooth (frictionless) slope?

This is a classic interview trap question. What does your intuition tell you? Think first — then pay attention to the key word in the question. (Note: the question is sometimes posed as "a frictionless slope" — but on a truly frictionless slope a ball cannot roll at all, since there is no friction to provide the torque. The intended comparison is rolling-on-rough vs sliding-on-smooth.)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — where the energy goes</b></summary>

The key difference lies in the energy partition:

- **Sliding** (frictionless incline): gravitational potential energy mgh is **entirely** converted to translational kinetic energy ½mv²
- **Rolling** (friction present to ensure pure rolling): gravitational potential energy mgh is converted to translational kinetic energy ½mv² **plus** rotational kinetic energy ½Iω²

When rolling, some energy is "wasted" on rotation!

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Quantitative comparison</b></summary>

For sliding: mgh = ½mvₛₗᵢdₑ² ⇒ vₛₗᵢdₑ = √2gh

For pure rolling (uniform solid sphere, I = ⅖mR², ω = vᵣₒₗₗ/R):

mgh = ½mvᵣₒₗₗ² + ½ · ⅖mR² · ((vᵣₒₗₗ)/(R))² = ½mvᵣₒₗₗ² + ⅕mvᵣₒₗₗ² = (7/10)mvᵣₒₗₗ²

vᵣₒₗₗ = √((10gh/7)) ≈ √(1.429gh)

Comparison: vₛₗᵢdₑ = √2gh > vᵣₒₗₗ = √(10gh/7)

**The sliding ball is faster!**

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: The "frictionless" trap in the question</b></summary>

Wait — the question says "frictionless slope". On a perfectly frictionless incline, a ball **cannot roll** — because there is no friction to provide the torque! So the ball would simply slide.

But the interviewer's intention is to compare "rolling on a rough incline" with "sliding on a smooth incline". If you spot the wording issue, the interviewer will be impressed — it shows your sensitivity to physical conditions.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

The sliding ball reaches the bottom faster. When rolling, some gravitational potential energy is converted to rotational kinetic energy, reducing the translational speed. vₛₗᵢdₑ = √2gh, whereas vᵣₒₗₗ = √(10gh/7) (for a uniform solid sphere).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach</b></summary>

"Don't use energy — resolve forces instead." For the sliding block, a = g sinθ. For the rolling sphere, friction f provides the torque: fR = Iα = (⅖mR²)(a/R), so f = ⅖ma. Along the slope, mg sinθ − f = ma, giving mg sinθ − ⅖ma = ma ⇒ a = (5/7)g sinθ. Since 5/7 ⋖ 1, the roller accelerates more slowly and is therefore slower at the bottom — the result is independent of the slope angle θ. This route is useful because it makes clear that friction, far from slowing the sphere, is what enables the rotation and so controls the speed indirectly.

</details>

---


---

### 1.2 Energy, Work & Power

---

#### 1.2.1 The hamburger energy problem

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A hamburger has 2.2 MJ of chemical potential energy. (a) How much energy to lift a 50 kg student 0.40 m? (b) How many step-ups to burn off one hamburger? (c) How many hamburgers to reach the top of Mount Everest (8.8 km)? (d) If a hamburger (0.22 kg) could propel itself, how high could it rise?

Four sub-problems, from simple to startling. Watch the units!

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — (a) and (b)</b></summary>

**(a)** E = mgh = 50 × 9.81 × 0.40 = 196.2 J ≈ 200 J

**(b)** Number of step-ups = (2.2 × 10⁶)/(196.2) ≈ 11,200

The interviewer may follow up: "What does this mean?" — 11,200 is the count of step-ups whose **mechanical** work totals one hamburger. The body, however, is only ~20–25 % efficient at turning metabolic energy into mechanical work, so each step-up actually costs ~800–1000 J metabolically. The honest answer is therefore 11,200 × (0.20–0.25) ≈ **2,200–2,800 step-ups**. (This is the same efficiency correction applied in C-Q66 for the Mars-bar question.)

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: (c) Reaching the summit of Everest</b></summary>

Eₜₒ ₜₒₚ = 50 × 9.81 × 8,800 = 4.32 × 10⁶ J ≈ 4.32 MJ

Number of hamburgers = 4.32 / 2.2 ≈ 1.96 ≈ 2 hamburgers

But the interviewer may follow up: "How many would you need in reality?" — far more than 2! Because this ignores basal metabolism (~2000 kcal/day ≈ 8.4 MJ/day), extra energy expenditure in the cold, reduced efficiency at high altitude due to hypoxia, etc. The pure potential energy calculation is a lower bound.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: (d) The self-propelling hamburger — a startling result</b></summary>

h = (E/mg) = (2.2 × 10⁶)/(0.22 × 9.81) = (2.2 × 10⁶)/(2.158) ≈ 1.02 × 10⁶ m ≈ 1,020 km

This far exceeds the height of the atmosphere (~100 km Kármán line)! The energy density of chemical energy is remarkably high — this is why fossil fuels and food can power our civilisation and our bodies.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

(a) ~200 J; (b) ~11,200 step-ups of mechanical work, i.e. ~2,200–2,800 once muscular efficiency (~20–25 %) is allowed for; (c) ~2 hamburgers (pure potential energy; more in practice); (d) ~1,020 km (far beyond the atmosphere).

</details>

---

#### 1.2.2 The pop-up toy

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A pop-up toy: head + sucker mass m = 1.5 kg on a light spring (natural length l₀ = 0.30 m, k = 250 N/m). Spring compressed to l₁ = 0.10 m. What height above ground does the bottom of the unstretched spring jump to when smoothly released?

Note: the question asks for the height of the **bottom of the spring** above the ground — not the height of the head. This adds complexity.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — an energy perspective</b></summary>

Spring compression x = l₀ - l₁ = 0.20 m.

Initial elastic potential energy: E_spring = ½kx² = ½ × 250 × 0.20² = 5.0 J

Initial gravitational potential energy (taking ground level as reference, head at height l₁ = 0.10 m): E_grav,i = mg · l₁ = 1.5 × 9.81 × 0.10 = 1.47 J

Initial total mechanical energy: E_total = 5.0 + 1.47 = 6.47 J

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: State when the spring returns to its natural length</b></summary>

When the spring returns to its natural length l₀ = 0.30 m, elastic potential energy is zero. The head is at height 0.30 m.

Kinetic energy of the head at this instant:

½mv² = E_total - mg · l₀ = 6.47 - 1.5 × 9.81 × 0.30 = 6.47 - 4.41 = 2.06 J

v² = (2 × 2.06)/(1.5) = 2.75 ⇒ v = 1.66 m/s

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 4: Motion after the spring base leaves the ground</b></summary>

While the spring is compressed it pushes the base **down** against the ground, so the base cannot move. The moment the spring reaches its natural length that push falls to zero; any further extension would *pull* the base upwards, and a light base offers no resistance — so lift-off occurs exactly there, with the head at 0.30 m and moving at v = 1.66 m/s.

Once airborne, both the spring and the base are light, so the spring force is zero throughout the flight: the spring simply keeps its natural length l₀ = 0.30 m, and head and base travel together as a projectile.

Using energy conservation, the extra rise is therefore:

h_extra = (v²)/(2g) = (2.75)/(2 × 9.81) = 0.140 m

Maximum head height = 0.30 + 0.140 = 0.44 m (above ground).

Since the spring stays at its natural length, the base is always 0.30 m below the head, so it peaks at 0.44 − 0.30 = **0.14 m** above the ground.

**⚠️ Subtlety worth flagging.** That clean result relies on the *massless* spring and base: a massless spring can exert no force once airborne, so it stays at natural length. Give the spring a finite mass and the head–base separation oscillates, so the base's peak height then depends on that internal oscillation. What is exact in every case is the centre-of-mass result: the CM rises a further v_cm²/(2g) = 0.14 m, to 0.44 m.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

Maximum height of the head ≈ 0.44 m; maximum height of the spring base ≈ 0.14 m. Core physics: elastic potential energy → kinetic energy + gravitational potential energy; the base lifts off the instant the spring reaches its natural length, after which head and base move together as a projectile.

</details>

---

#### 1.2.3 Car power and resistive forces

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A car of mass 1000 kg travels at steady 20 m/s on a level road; engine working at 18 kW. (a) Find resistive force. (b) Engine disconnected, brakes applied, car stops in 50 m — find braking force. (c) Same on upward incline (sinθ = 1/20). (d) Same on downward incline.

Four scenarios, increasing in complexity. Start with the simplest: constant-velocity case.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — (a) Find the resistance / drag</b></summary>

Constant velocity → driving force = resistive force.

Power P = Fv (when force and velocity are in the same direction):

Fᵣₑₛᵢₛₜ = (P/v) = (18,000/20) = 900 N

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: (b) Braking force</b></summary>

Initial velocity v = 20 m/s, braking distance s = 50 m, final velocity 0.

v² = 2as ⇒ a = (v²)/(2s) = (400/100) = 4 m/s²

Net force = ma = 1000 × 4 = 4,000 N (opposite to the direction of motion).

This net force = braking force + natural resistance (natural resistance still acts after the engine is disconnected):

F_brake + 900 = 4,000 ⇒ F_brake = 3,100 N

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 4: (c) Uphill and (d) downhill</b></summary>

**(c) Uphill**: read (c) and (d) as *coasting* distances — engine disconnected and **no braking**, so the only retarding forces are drag and gravity. (If the brakes stayed on, the 3,100 N brake force from (b) would have to be added too.)

Extra resistance = mgsinθ = 1000 × 9.81 × (1/20) = 490.5 N

Total resistance = 900 + 490.5 = 1,390.5 N

Braking distance (engine disconnected, no braking): ½mv² = Fᵣₑₛᵢₛₜ × s

s = (1000 × 400)/(2 × 1390.5) = 144 m

**(d) Downhill**: the component of weight along the slope does positive work.

Net resistance = 900 - 490.5 = 409.5 N

s = (200,000/409.5) = 488 m

The braking distance is longer downhill — gravity "helps" the car along.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

(a) 900 N; (b) 3,100 N; (c) uphill ≈ 144 m; (d) downhill ≈ 488 m.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✨ A Sharper Route — one work balance for all four parts</b></summary>

**Why it is worth knowing:** the worked solution uses three different methods across (a)–(d). In the interview that reads as four separate problems; in fact they are one.

**Key insight:** after part (a), every part is the same statement — the initial kinetic energy is removed by the **total** retarding work, including gravity on a slope.

**Steps:**

1. Level road: `F_net · s = ½mv²`, so `F_net = mv²/(2s) = 4000 N`. Subtract the 900 N drag → brake force **3100 N**.
2. Uphill, coasting (no brakes): drag plus gravity gives `900 + 490.5 = 1390.5 N`, so `s = 200000/1390.5 ≈ 144 m`. Note the brake force from (b) is *not* included — that is what makes this a coasting distance.
3. Downhill, coasting: gravity subtracts, giving `900 − 490.5 = 409.5 N` and `s ≈ 488 m`.
4. Notice that mass cancelled in step 1 — the stopping distance depends on `u²`, not on how heavy the car is.

**Result:** 900 N; 3100 N; 144 m uphill; 488 m downhill.

**Why it is better:** one pattern covers (b), (c) and (d); no deceleration is ever computed, and the two slope cases differ only by a sign in the same equation.

</details>

---

#### 1.2.4 Uniform rod released from horizontal — find angular velocity at the vertical

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer describes the scenario</b></summary>

**Problem**: A uniform rod of mass m and length L is held horizontally, pivoted at one end, and released from rest. Find the angular velocity when it reaches the vertical position.

The rod rotates about one end — how many methods can you think of to solve this?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — the energy method (most elegant)</b></summary>

Moment of inertia of a rod about one end: I = ⅓mL² (this is a standard result; the interviewer expects you to know it or be able to derive it).

Take the pivot as the zero of potential energy.

Initial (horizontal): centre of mass at pivot height, Uᵢ = 0, Kᵢ = 0

Final (vertical, downwards): centre of mass L/2 below the pivot, U_f = -mg(L/2)

Energy conservation: 0 = -mg(L/2) + ½Iω²

mg(L/2) = ½ · ⅓mL² · ω² ⇒ ω² = (3g/L) ⇒ ω = √((3g/L))

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 3: Verification and discussion</b></summary>

Dimensional check: [ω] = √([L/T²]/[L]) = [1/T] ✓

Extreme cases: L → ∞ ⇒ ω → 0 (a long rod rotates slowly) ✓; g → 0 ⇒ ω → 0 (no gravity, no rotation) ✓

The interviewer may follow up: "Can you do it using Newton's second law (torque)?"

Yes — but fix the angle convention first. Let θ be measured **from the downward vertical**; then τ = −mg(L/2) sinθ and \ddot{θ} = −(3g/2L) sinθ (a nonlinear differential equation). The minus sign is what makes it a restoring torque; if instead you measure θ from the horizontal, write cosθ in place of sinθ. The energy method avoids the integration altogether — this is the wisdom of choosing the right method.

The interviewer may also ask about the reaction force at the pivot: centre-of-mass acceleration a_c = ω²(L/2) = 3g/2, N - mg = m · 3g/2 ⇒ N = 5mg/2.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

ω = √(3g/L). The energy method (using the moment of inertia about one end, I = mL²/3) is the most elegant approach.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Alternative Approach: the parallel-axis theorem shortcut</b></summary>

The energy method in the main solution uses I = ⅓mL² (moment of inertia about the end). An elegant alternative is to use the **parallel-axis theorem** to build this from the more familiar centre-of-mass moment of inertia:

I_end = I_cm + m(L/2)² = (1/12)mL² + (1/4)mL² = (1/3)mL²

This is worth doing in the interview because it shows you can *derive* the end-pivot moment of inertia rather than quote it, and it connects to the deeper point that the rod's motion is a *rotation about the pivot* (not about the centre of mass).

A second, more physical alternative is to note that the centre of mass falls a distance L/2, so by energy conservation ½mv_cm² + ½I_cm ω² = mg(L/2), with v_cm = ω(L/2). Substituting I_cm = (1/12)mL²:

½m(ωL/2)² + ½(1/12)mL²ω² = mg(L/2)
⇒ (1/8 + 1/24)mL²ω² = mg(L/2)
⇒ (1/6)mL²ω² = mg(L/2)
⇒ ω² = 3g/L

This "split the kinetic energy into translation + rotation about the centre of mass" approach is more general and reinforces the parallel-axis theorem. Both routes give ω = √(3g/L).

</details>

---

### 1.3 Rotational Motion & Orbits

---

#### 1.3.1 Planetary Orbits — Deriving the Circular Motion Equations

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer asks for a derivation</b></summary>

**Question**: Questions looking at planetary orbits, asking you to derive some equations of circular motion.

The interviewer expects you to start from first principles — the law of gravitation and Newton's second law.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — Gravity and centripetal force</b></summary>

Assume the planet moves in uniform circular motion. Gravity supplies the centripetal force:

F_gravity = (GMm)/(r²) F_centripetal = (mv²)/(r)

Set them equal:

(GMm)/(r²) = (mv²)/(r) ⇒ v² = (GM/r)

Notice: m cancels! Orbital speed is independent of the planet's mass — this is the Newtonian statement of **universal free fall**: the same m appears both as gravitational charge and as inertial resistance. It is tempting to invoke Einstein's equivalence principle here; resist it, because nothing relativistic is happening and an interviewer will notice the overreach.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>📐 Step 3: Deriving the period — Kepler's third law</b></summary>

T = (2π r)/(v) = (2π r)/(√(GM/r)) = 2π√((r³)/(GM))

T² = (4π²)/(GM)r³

This is **Kepler's third law**! The interviewer expects you to recognise this — Newton "derived" Kepler's empirical law from the laws of gravitation and motion, one of the greatest unifications in the history of physics.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>🔬 Step 4: Discussion — domain of applicability</b></summary>

- T² ∝ r³: larger orbital radius → longer period
- M appears in the denominator: more massive central body → shorter period
- This derivation holds only for **circular orbits**. For elliptical orbits, r should be replaced by the **semi-major axis** a: T² = 4π² a³/(GM)
- If the two bodies have comparable masses (e.g. binary stars), a reduced-mass correction is needed

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>✅ Final answer</b></summary>

v = √(GM/r); T² = 4π² r³/(GM) (Kepler's third law, circular-orbit form).

</details>

---

#### 1.3.2 Why Is It Not Strictly True to Say One Planet Orbits Another?

<details>
<summary><b>🔍 Step 1: The interviewer challenges your common sense</b></summary>

**Question**: Why is it not strictly true to say that one planet orbits another?

We often say "the Earth goes round the Sun" — what is wrong with that?

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>💡 Step 2: Guidance — the essence of the two-body problem</b></summary>

Strictly, both bodies orbit their **common centre of mass** (barycentre). Only when one body is far more massive than the other does the barycentre nearly coincide with the centre of the larger body.

Barycentre position: let the two masses be M and m, separated by distance r.
